One can generalize these quivers of type
\mathsf{ADE}
as follows:

Definition 5.1

(Tripod quiver)
.

For
a,b,c\geq 1
, the
tripod

\mathcal{Q}(a,b,c)
of type
(a,b,c)
is a bipartite quiver such that

(1)

the set of vertices is
[n]
for
n=a+b+c-2
,

(2)

the underlying graph is a boundary wedge sum of three quivers
\mathsf{A}_{a},\mathsf{A}_{b}
, and
\mathsf{A}_{c}
, and

(3)

the vertex where
\mathsf{A}_{a},\mathsf{A}_{b}
, and
\mathsf{A}_{c}
are glued together is called the
central vertex
, labelled as
1
and colored as
+
.

We define an
N
-graph
\mathscr{G}(a,b,c)
on
\mathbb{D}^{2}
as the concatenation of three
N
-graphs
\mathscr{G}(A_{a}),\mathscr{G}(A_{b})
and
\mathscr{G}(A_{c})
by making one
\mathsf{Y}
-cycle
\gamma_{1}
and define a good tuple
\mathscr{B}(a,b,c)
of cycles as the union of cycles in three
N
-graphs.
The
N
-graph obtained by switching colors from
\mathscr{G}(a,b,c)
and the induced set of chosen cycles will be denoted by
\bar{\mathscr{G}}(a,b,c)
and
\bar{\mathscr{B}}(a,b,c)
, respectively.

The pictorial definitions of
\mathcal{Q}(a,b,c),\mathscr{G}(a,b,c)
and
\mathscr{B}(a,b,c)
are depicted in Figure
25
.

It is obvious that if
a,b
or
c
is one, then it is the same as
\mathsf{A}_{n}
for
n=a+b+c-2
up to relabelling.
Similarly, the cases that
a,b
or
c
is two include quivers of type
\mathsf{D}_{n}
and
\mathsf{E}_{n}
.

Notice that the boundary of the Legendrian weave
\Lambda(\mathscr{G}(a,b,c))
denoted by
\lambda(a,b,c)
is expressed as the braid word

\displaystyle\lambda(a,b,c)

\displaystyle=\cl(\beta(a,b,c)),

\displaystyle\beta(a,b,c)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}^{a+1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}^{b+1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}^{c+1}.

Then this braid is equivalent to the following:

\displaystyle\beta(a,b,c)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}^{a+1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}^{b+1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}^{c+1}=\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}(\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1})\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{1}^{b-1}\sigma_{2}^{c}(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2})\sigma_{1}=\Delta\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{1}^{b-1}\sigma_{2}^{c}\Delta.

Hence
\lambda(a,b,c)\subset J^{1}\mathbb{S}^{1}
corresponds to the rainbow closure of the braid
\beta_{0}(a,b,c)=\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{a}\sigma_{1}^{b-1}\sigma_{2}^{c}
.

Then this quiver
\mathcal{Q}^{\mathsf{brick}}(a,b,c)
is obviously mutation equivalent to the bipartite quiver
\mathcal{Q}(a,b,c)
.

It is not hard to check that
\beta(1,b,c)
is a stabilization of
\beta(\mathsf{A}_{n})=\sigma_{1}^{n+3}
for
n=b+c-1
since

\displaystyle\beta(\mathsf{A}_{n})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}^{n+3}=\sigma_{1}^{b+1}\sigma_{1}^{c+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm{(S)}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}^{2}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}^{b+1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}^{c+1}=\beta(1,b,c).

Lemma 5.3
.

The
N
-graph
\mathscr{G}(1,b,c)
is a stabilization of
\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{A}_{n})
for
n=b+c-1
.

Proof.

According to Remark
2.7
and Figure
9
, a stabilization of
\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{A}_{n})
is given as the second picture in Figure
26
.
Then by adding an annular
N
-graph corresponding to a sequence of
{\rm{(RIII)}}
, we obtain the third, which produces the fourth by applying the following generalized push-through move.

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm{(II^{*})}}}

Now we add an annular
N
-graph consisting of
{\rm{(RIII)}}
’s as above to obtain the fifth
N
-graph in Figure
26
, which is the same as
\mathscr{G}(1,b,c)
as desired up to Move (II) at the center.
∎

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm{(II^{*})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm{(II)}}}

Figure 26.
The
N
-graph
\mathscr{G}(1,b,c)
is a stabilization of
\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{A}_{n})
for
n=b+c-1

Definition 5.4
.

Let
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
and
(\mathscr{G}^{\prime},\mathscr{B}^{\prime})
be pairs of
N
-graphs and good tuples of cycles.
We say that
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
is
Legendrian mutation equivalent
to
(\mathscr{G}^{\prime},\mathscr{B}^{\prime})
if there exists a sequence
\mu
of Legendrian mutations which sends
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
to
(\mathscr{G}^{\prime},\mathscr{B}^{\prime})
up to equivalence. That is,

[\mu(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})]=[(\mathscr{G}^{\prime},\mathscr{B}^{\prime})].

In particular,
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
is said to be
of type
\mathsf{X}
or
of type
(a,b,c)
if it is Legendrian mutation equivalent to
(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{X}),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{X}))
or
(\mathscr{G}(a,b,c),\mathscr{B}(a,b,c))
, respectively.

5.2.
Coxeter mutation for tripods

For a bipartite quiver
\mathcal{Q}
, we have two sets of vertices
I_{+}
and

I_{-}
so that all edges are oriented from
I_{+}
to
I_{-}
.
By definition, for a tripod
\mathcal{Q}(a,b,c)
, the central vertex
1
is lying in
I_{+}
.
Let
\mu_{+}
and
\mu_{-}
be sequences of mutations defined by
compositions of mutations corresponding to each and every vertex in
I_{+}
and

I_{-}
, respectively.
A Coxeter mutation
\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}
is the composition

\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}=\mu_{-}\mu_{+}=\prod_{i\in I_{-}}\mu_{i}\cdot\prod_{i\in I_{+}}\mu_{i}.

Remark 5.5
.

For any sequence
\mu
of mutations, we will use the right-to-left convention. Namely, the rightmost mutation will be applied first on the quiver
\mathcal{Q}
.

Similarly, we define the Legendrian Coxeter mutation, which will be denoted by
\mu_{\mathscr{G}}
, on a bipartite
N
-graph
\mathscr{G}
as follows:

Definition 5.6

(Legendrian Coxeter mutation)
.

For a bipartite
N
-graph
\mathscr{G}
with decomposed sets of cycles
\mathscr{B}=\mathscr{B}_{+}\cup\mathscr{B}_{-}
, we define the
Legendrian Coxeter mutation

\mu_{\mathscr{G}}
as the composition of Legendrian mutations

\mu_{\mathscr{G}}=\prod_{\gamma\in\mathscr{B}_{-}}\mu_{\gamma}\cdot\prod_{\gamma\in\mathscr{B}_{+}}\mu_{\gamma}.

Lemma 5.7
.

The effect of the Legendrian Coxeter mutation on
(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{A}_{n}),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{A}_{n}))
is the clockwise
\frac{2\pi}{n+3}
-rotation.

Proof.

We may assume that the Coxeter element
\mu_{\mathscr{G}}
can be represented by the sequence

\mu_{\mathscr{G}}=\mu_{-}\mu_{+}=(\mu_{\gamma_{2}}\mu_{\gamma_{4}}\mu_{\gamma_{6}}\cdots)(\mu_{\gamma_{1}}\mu_{\gamma_{3}}\mu_{\gamma_{5}}\dots).

Then the action of
\mu_{\mathscr{G}}
on
\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{A}_{n})
is as depicted in Figure
27
, which is nothing but the clockwise
\frac{2\pi}{n+3}
-rotation of the original
N
-graph
(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{A}_{n}),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{A}_{n}))
as claimed.
∎

\cdots
(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{A}_{n}),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{A}_{n}))
\cdots
\mu_{+}(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{A}_{n}),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{A}_{n}))

\cdots

\mu_{\mathscr{G}}(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{A}_{n}),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{A}_{n}))
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{-}}

Figure 27.
Legendrian Coxeter mutation
\mu_{\mathscr{G}}
on
(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{A}_{n}),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{A}_{n}))

Remark 5.8
.

The order of the Coxeter mutation is either
(n+3)/2
if
n
is odd or
n+3
otherwise.
Since the Coxeter number
h=n+1
for
\mathsf{A}_{n}
, this verifies Lemma
3.20
.

Let
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B},\mathcal{F}_{\lambda})
be a triple of a good
N
-graph, a good tuple of cycles and flags
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}
on
\lambda
.
Suppose that the quiver
\mathcal{Q}(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
is bipartite and
\mu_{\mathscr{G}}(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
is well-defined.
Then by Proposition
4.19
, we have

\Psi(\mu_{\mathscr{G}}(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B}),\mathcal{F}_{\lambda})=\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}(\Psi(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B},\mathcal{F}_{\lambda})).

In particular, for quivers of type
\mathsf{A}_{n}
or tripods we have the following corollary.

Corollary 5.9
.

For each
n\geq 1
and
a,b,c\geq 1
, the Legendrian Coxeter mutation
\mu_{\mathscr{G}}
on
(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{A}_{n}),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{A}_{n}))
or
(\mathscr{G}(a,b,c),\mathscr{B}(a,b,c))
corresponds to the Coxeter mutation
\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}
on
\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{A}_{n})
or
\mathcal{Q}(a,b,c)
, respectively.

By the mutation convention mentioned above, for each tripod
\mathscr{G}(a,b,c)
, we always take a mutation at the central
\mathsf{Y}
-cycle
\gamma_{1}
first.
After the Legendrian mutation on
(\mathscr{G}(a,b,c),\mathscr{B}(a,b,c))
at
\gamma_{1}
, we have the
N
-graph on the left in Figure
28(a)
.
Then there are three shaded regions that we can apply the generalized push-through moves so that we obtain the
N
-graph on the right in Figure
28(a)
.

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm{(II^{*})}}}

(a)

After the mutation at the central vertex

(b)

After Legendrian Coxeter mutation

Figure 28.
Legendrian Coxeter mutation for
(\mathscr{G}(a,b,c),\mathscr{B}(a,b,c))

Notice that in each triangular shaded region, the
N
-subgraph looks like the
N
-graph of type
\mathsf{A}_{a-1},\mathsf{A}_{b-1}
or
\mathsf{A}_{c-1}
.
Moreover, the mutations corresponding to the rest sequence is just a composition
of Coxeter mutations of type
\mathsf{A}_{a-1},\mathsf{A}_{b-1}
and
\mathsf{A}_{c-1}
,
which are essentially the same as the clock wise rotations.
Therefore, the result of the Coxeter mutation will be given as depicted in
Figure
28(b)
.

Then one can observe that this is very similar to the original
N
-graph
\mathscr{G}(a,b,c)
.
Indeed, the inside is identical to
\mathscr{G}(a,b,c)
but the colors are switched, which is
\bar{\mathscr{G}}(a,b,c)
by definition.
The complement of
\bar{\mathscr{G}}(a,b,c)
in
\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathscr{G}(a,b,c),\mathscr{B}(a,b,c))
is an annular
N
-graph.

Definition 5.10

(Coxeter padding)
.

For each triple
a,b,c
, the annular
N
-graph depicted in Figure
29
is denoted by
\mathscr{C}(a,b,c)
and called the
Coxeter padding
of type
(a,b,c)
.
We also denote the Coxeter padding with color switched by
\bar{\mathscr{C}}(a,b,c)
.

(a)

\mathscr{C}(a,b,c)

(b)

\bar{\mathscr{C}}(a,b,c)

Figure 29.
Coxeter paddings
\mathscr{C}(a,b,c)
and
\bar{\mathscr{C}}(a,b,c)

Notice that two Coxeter paddings
\mathscr{C}(a,b,c)
and
\bar{\mathscr{C}}(a,b,c)
can be glued without any ambiguity
and so we can also pile up Coxeter paddings
\mathscr{C}(a,b,c)
and
\bar{\mathscr{C}}(a,b,c)
alternatively as many times as we want.

We also define the concatenation of the Coxeter padding
\bar{\mathscr{C}}(a,b,c)
on the pair
(\mathscr{G}(a,b,c),\mathscr{B}(a,b,c))
as the pair
(\mathscr{G}^{\prime},\mathscr{B}^{\prime})
such that

(1)

the
N
-graph
\mathscr{G}^{\prime}
is obtained by gluing
\bar{\mathscr{C}}(a,b,c)
on
\mathscr{G}(a,b,c)
, and

(2)

the tuple
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
of cycles is the set of
\mathsf{I}
- and
\mathsf{Y}
-cycles identified with
\mathscr{B}(a,b,c)
in a canonical way.

Proposition 5.11
.

The Legendrian Coxeter mutation on
(\mathscr{G}(a,b,c),\mathscr{B}(a,b,c))
or
(\bar{\mathscr{G}}(a,b,c),\bar{\mathscr{B}}(a,b,c))
is given as the concatenation

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathscr{G}}(\mathscr{G}(a,b,c),\mathscr{B}(a,b,c))

\displaystyle=\mathscr{C}(a,b,c)(\bar{\mathscr{G}}(a,b,c),\bar{\mathscr{B}}(a,b,c)),

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathscr{G}}(\bar{\mathscr{G}}(a,b,c),\mathscr{B}(a,b,c))

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathscr{C}}(a,b,c)(\mathscr{G}(a,b,c),\mathscr{B}(a,b,c)).

Proof.

This follows directly from the above observation.
∎

It is important that this proposition holds only when we take the Legendrian Coxeter mutation on the very standard
N
-graph with the tuple of cycles
(\mathscr{G}(a,b,c),\mathscr{B}(a,b,c))
.
Otherwise, the Legendrian Coxeter mutation will not be expressed as simple as above.

Theorem 5.12
.

For
a,b,c\geq 1
with
\frac{1}{a}+\frac{1}{b}+\frac{1}{c}\leq 1
,
The Legendrian knot or link
\lambda(a,b,c)
in
J^{1}\mathbb{S}^{1}
admits infinitely many distinct exact embedded Lagrangian fillings.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.11
, the effect of the Legendrian Coxeter mutation on
(\mathscr{G}(a,b,c),\mathscr{B}(a,b,c))
is just to attach the Coxeter padding on
(\bar{\mathscr{G}}(a,b,c),\bar{\mathscr{B}}(a,b,c))
.
In particular, as mentioned earlier, for each
r\geq 0
, the iterated Legendrian Coxeter mutation

\mu_{\mathscr{G}}^{r}(\mathscr{G}(a,b,c),\mathscr{B}(a,b,c))

is well-defined.
Each of these
N
-graphs define a Legendrian weave
\Lambda(\mu_{\mathscr{G}}^{r}(\mathscr{G}(a,b,c),\mathscr{B}(a,b,c)))
, whose Lagrangian projection is a Lagrangian filling

L_{r}(a,b,c)\coloneqq(\pi\circ\iota)(\Lambda(\mu_{\mathscr{G}}^{r}(\mathscr{G}(a,b,c),\mathscr{B}(a,b,c)))

as desired. Therefore it suffices to prove that Lagrangians
L_{r}(a,b,c)
for
r\geq 0
are pairwise distinct up to exact Lagrangian isotopy, when
\frac{1}{a}+\frac{1}{b}+\frac{1}{c}\leq 1
.

Now suppose that
\frac{1}{a}+\frac{1}{b}+\frac{1}{c}\leq 1
, or equivalently,
\mathcal{Q}(a,b,c)
is of infinite type.
Then the order of the Coxeter mutation is infinite by Lemma
3.20
and so is the order of the Legendrian Coxeter mutation by Corollary
5.9
.
In particular, for fixed flags
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}
on
\lambda
, the set

\left\{\Psi(\mu_{\mathscr{G}}^{r}(\mathscr{G}(a,b,c),\mathscr{B}(a,b,c)),\mathcal{F}_{\lambda})\mid r\geq 0\right\}

is the set of infinitely many pairwise distinct seeds in the cluster pattern for
\mathcal{Q}(a,b,c)
.
Hence by Corollary
4.15
, we have pairwise distinct Lagrangian fillings
L_{r}(a,b,c)
.
∎

5.3.
N
-graphs of type
\mathsf{ADE}

In this section, we will prove one of the main theorem.

Theorem 5.13
.

Let
\lambda
be a Legendrian knot or link which is either
\lambda(\mathsf{A}_{n})
or
\lambda(a,b,c)
of type
\mathsf{ADE}
.
Then it admits exact embedded Lagrangian fillings as many as seeds in its seed pattern of the same type.

Indeed, this theorem follows from the generalized questions.

Question 5.14
.

For given
N
-graph
\mathscr{G}
with a chosen set
\mathscr{B}
of cycles, can we take a Legendrian mutation as many times as we want? Or equivalently, after applying a mutation
\mu_{k}
on
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
, is the tuple
\mu_{k}(\mathscr{B})
still good in
\mu_{k}(\mathscr{G})
?

This question has been raised previously in
[
8
, Remark 7.13]
.
One of the main reason making the question nontrivial is that
the potential difference of geometric and algebraic intersections between two cycles.
More concretely, two cycles
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
as shown in Figure
30
, can never be isotoped off to each other but their signed intersections following the rule in Figure
22
vanishes.
Hence in the corresponding quiver to the first local
N
-graph, there are no arrows between the corresponding vertices
1
and
2
.
However, after a sequence of Move
{\rm{(II)}}
, we can deform
\gamma_{2}
into
\gamma(e)
for an edge
e
as depicted in the third picture of Figure
30
.
The mutation
\mu_{\gamma(e)}
transforms
\gamma_{1}
to
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}
, which is
not
good and so it is not clear how to define a mutation
\mu_{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}}
.

\gamma_{1}
\gamma_{2}
\gamma_{2}
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}
\gamma_{2}^{\prime}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm{(II)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm{(II)}}\circ{\rm{(II)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\gamma_{2}}}

Figure 30.
Non-disjoint cycles
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
with signed intersection number zero, and a mutation
\mu_{e}
.

Instead of attacking this question directly, we will prove the following:

Proposition 5.15
.

Let
\lambda
be as above and
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}
be flags on
\lambda
.
Suppose that
\Sigma
is a seed in the seed pattern of the same type with the initial seed

\Sigma_{t_{0}}=\begin{cases}\Psi(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{A}_{n}),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{A}_{n}),\mathcal{F}_{\lambda})&\lambda=\lambda(\mathsf{A}_{n});\\
\Psi(\mathscr{G}(a,b,c),\mathscr{B}(a,b,c),\mathcal{F}_{\lambda})&\lambda=\lambda(a,b,c).\end{cases}

Then
\lambda
admits either an
N
-graph
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
on
\mathbb{D}^{2}
such that

\mathscr{G}
is either a
2
-graph if
\lambda=\lambda(\mathsf{A}_{n})
or a
3
-graph if
\lambda=\lambda(a,b,c)
, and

\Sigma=\Psi(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B},\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}).

Under the aid of this proposition, one can prove Theorem
5.13
.

Proof of Theorem
5.13
.

Let
\lambda
be given as above.
Then by Proposition
5.15
, we have pairs of
N
-graphs and good tuples of cycles which have a one-to-one correspondence
\Psi
with seeds in the seed pattern of
\mathcal{Q}(a,b,c)
.
Hence any pair of the Lagrangian fillings coming from these
N
-graphs is never exact Lagrangian isotopic by Corollary
4.15
.
This completes the proof.
∎

We will use the following observations: let
P(\Phi)
be the generalized associahedron for the root system
\Phi
of type
\mathsf{ADE}
(cf. Theorem
3.7
and §
3.4
).

(1)

There is one-to-one correspondence between the sets of vertices and seeds.

(2)

There is one-to-one correspondence between the set of facets, faces of codimension 1, and the set of almost positive roots
\Phi_{\geq-1}
.

(3)

For the initial seed
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
, we may assume that the facets of codimension one including
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
correspond to negative simple roots. Namely, there are exactly
n
-facets

\mathscr{F}=\{F_{-\alpha_{i}}\mid\alpha_{i}\in\Pi\}.

(4)

The orbits of
\mathscr{F}
under the action of the Legendrian Coxeter mutation
\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}
exhaust all facets.

Proof of Proposition
5.15
.

For a Legendrian link
\lambda
of type
\mathsf{ADE}
, we fix flags
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}
on
\lambda
.
Let us define the initial
2
-graph or
3
-graph with the chosen tuple of cycles
(\mathscr{G}_{t_{0}},\mathscr{B}_{t_{0}})
as

(\mathscr{G}_{t_{0}},\mathscr{B}_{t_{0}})=\begin{cases}(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{A}_{n}),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{A}_{n}))&\lambda=\lambda(\mathsf{A}_{n});\\
(\mathscr{G}(a,b,c),\mathscr{B}(a,b,c))&\lambda=\lambda(a,b,c),\end{cases}

which defines the initial seed
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
via
\Psi

\displaystyle\Sigma_{t_{0}}=\Psi(\mathscr{G}_{t_{0}},\mathscr{B}_{t_{0}},\mathcal{F}_{\lambda})=(\mathbf{x}(\Lambda(\mathscr{G}_{t_{0}}),\mathscr{B}_{t_{0}},\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}),\mathcal{Q}(\Lambda(\mathscr{G}_{t_{0}}),\mathscr{B}_{t_{0}})).

Suppose that
\Sigma
is a seed in the cluster pattern.
Then we need to to prove that there exists an
N
-graph
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
such that
\Psi(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B},\mathcal{F}_{\lambda})=\Sigma
.

By Proposition
3.15
and Lemma
3.16
, there exist an integer
r
and a sequence
\mu^{\prime}
of mutations such that

\Sigma=\mu^{\prime}(\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{r}(\Sigma_{t_{0}})),

where
\mu^{\prime}
joins
\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{r}(\Sigma_{t_{0}})
and
\Sigma
inside a facet.

If
\lambda=\lambda(\mathsf{A}_{n})
, then
\mu_{\mathscr{G}}
acts on
(\mathscr{G}_{t_{0}},\mathscr{B}_{t_{0}})
as the
\left(\frac{2\pi}{n+2}\right)
-rotation, which obviously commutes with Legendrian mutation
\mu^{\prime}
.
Hence it suffices to show the well-definedness of
\mu^{\prime}(\mathscr{G}_{t_{0}},\mathscr{B}_{t_{0}})
.

Otherwise, as seen earlier, the action of the Legendrian Coxeter mutation
\mu_{\mathscr{G}}^{r}
on
(\mathscr{G}_{t_{0}},\mathscr{B}_{t_{0}})
is obtained by the concatenation of sequences of
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}(a,b,c)
and
\bar{\mathscr{C}}=\bar{\mathscr{C}}(a,b,c)
to either
(\mathscr{G}_{t_{0}},\mathscr{B}_{t_{0}})
or
(\bar{\mathscr{G}}_{t_{0}},\bar{\mathscr{B}}_{t_{0}})
.

\mu_{\mathscr{G}}^{r}(\mathscr{G}_{t_{0}},\mathscr{B}_{t_{0}})=\begin{cases}\mathscr{C}\bar{\mathscr{C}}\cdots\bar{\mathscr{C}}(\mathscr{G}_{t_{0}},\mathscr{B}_{t_{0}})&r\text{ is even},\\
\mathscr{C}\bar{\mathscr{C}}\cdots\mathscr{C}(\bar{\mathscr{G}}_{t_{0}},\bar{\mathscr{B}}_{t_{0}})&r\text{ is odd}.\end{cases}

Let us regard the sequence
\mu^{\prime}
of mutations as the sequence of Legendrian mutations.
Since the concatenation of
\mathscr{C}
or
\bar{\mathscr{C}}
do not touch any chosen cycle in
\mathscr{B}_{t_{0}}
, two operations—the concatenation of
\mathscr{C}
or
\bar{\mathscr{C}}
, and the mutation
\mu^{\prime}
— commute.
Therefore

\mu^{\prime}(\mu_{\mathscr{G}}^{r}(\mathscr{G}_{t_{0}},\mathscr{B}_{t_{0}}))=\begin{cases}\mathscr{C}\bar{\mathscr{C}}\cdots\bar{\mathscr{C}}(\mu^{\prime}(\mathscr{G}_{t_{0}},\mathscr{B}_{t_{0}}))&r\text{ is even},\\
\mathscr{C}\bar{\mathscr{C}}\cdots\mathscr{C}(\mu^{\prime}(\bar{\mathscr{G}}_{t_{0}},\bar{\mathscr{B}}_{t_{0}}))&r\text{ is odd},\end{cases}

and the proposition follows if
\mu^{\prime}(\mathscr{G}_{t_{0}},\mathscr{B}_{t_{0}})
and
\mu^{\prime}(\bar{\mathscr{G}}_{t_{0}},\bar{\mathscr{B}}_{t_{0}})
are well-defined.
Since
\mathscr{G}_{t_{0}}
and
\bar{\mathscr{G}}_{t_{0}}
are essentially the same, it suffices to show the well-definedness of
\mu^{\prime}(\mathscr{G}_{t_{0}},\mathscr{B}_{t_{0}})
as before.

Now we will prove the well-definedness of
\mu^{\prime}(\mathscr{G}_{t_{0}},\mathscr{B}_{t_{0}})
in both cases by using induction on
n
.
Suppose that
\mu^{\prime}
is a sequence of mutations in a facet
F_{\beta}
for some
\beta\in\Phi_{\geq-1}
, and
\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{r}(F_{-\alpha_{i}})=F_{\beta}
for some
\alpha_{i}\in\Pi
.
Then the facet
F_{\beta}
is combinatorially equivalent to the lower dimensional
generalized associahedron

F_{\beta}\cong P(\Phi([n]\setminus\{i\}))\cong P(\Phi_{1})\times\cdots\times P(\Phi_{m}).

Here,
\Phi([n]\setminus\{i\})
is not necessarily irreducible
and we denote by
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{\ell},\ell\leq 3
the root systems
satisfying that
\Phi([n]\setminus\{i\})=\Phi_{1}\times\cdots\times\Phi_{\ell}
.
Moreover, in terms of quivers, if we denote the connected components of
\mathcal{Q}\setminus\{i\}
by
\mathcal{Q}^{(1)},\dots,\mathcal{Q}^{(\ell)}
, then we may say that
\Phi_{j}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{(j)}
are of the same type.
Therefore the sequence
\mu^{\prime}
of mutations can be decomposed into
\mu^{(1)},\dots,\mu^{(\ell)}
on
\mathcal{Q}^{(1)},\dots,\mathcal{Q}^{(\ell)}
, respectively.

Similarly, in
N
-graph
\mathscr{G}_{t_{0}}
, the
i
-th cycle
[\gamma_{i}]
separates
(\mathscr{G}_{t_{0}},\mathscr{B}_{t_{0}})
into at most three parts
\{(\mathscr{G}_{1},\mathscr{B}_{1}),\dots,(\mathscr{G}_{\ell},\mathscr{B}_{\ell})\}
, as seen in Figure
31
.
This means that

\mu^{(j)}(\mathscr{G}_{j},\mathscr{B}_{j})\text{ is well-defined for all }1\leq j\leq\ell\Longrightarrow\mu^{\prime}(\mathscr{G}_{t_{0}},\mathscr{B}_{t_{0}})\text{ is well-defined}.

Indeed, if
\lambda=\lambda(\mathsf{A}_{n})
, then we have the following two cases:

(1)

if
\gamma_{i}
corresponds to a bivalent vertex, then for some
1\leq r,s
with
r+s+1=n
, we have two
2
-subgraphs

\{(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{A}_{r}),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{A}_{r})),(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{A}_{s}),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{A}_{s}))\};

(2)

if
\gamma_{i}
corresponds to a leaf, then we have the
2
-subgraph

\{(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{A}_{n-1}),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{A}_{n-1})\}.

Otherwise, if
\lambda=\lambda(a,b,c)
, then we have the following three cases:

(1)

if
\gamma_{i}
corresponds to the central vertex, then we have three
3
-subgraphs

\{(\mathscr{G}_{(3)}(\mathsf{A}_{a-1}),\mathscr{B}_{(3)}(\mathsf{A}_{a-1})),(\mathscr{G}_{(3)}(\mathsf{A}_{b-1}),\mathscr{B}_{(3)}(\mathsf{A}_{b-1})),(\mathscr{G}_{(3)}(\mathsf{A}_{c-1}),\mathscr{B}_{(3)}(\mathsf{A}_{c-1}))\},

(2)

if
\gamma_{i}
corresponds to a bivalent vertex, then for some
1\leq r,s
with
r+s+1=a
, up to permuting indices
a,b,c
, we have two
3
-subgraphs

\{(\mathscr{G}_{(3)}(\mathsf{A}_{s}),\mathscr{B}_{(3)}(\mathsf{A}_{s})),(\mathscr{G}(r,b,c),\mathscr{B}(r,b,c))\},

(3)

otherwise, if
\gamma_{i}
corresponds to a leaf, then up to permuting indices
a,b,c
, we have the
3
-subgraph

\{(\mathscr{G}(a-1,b,c),\mathscr{B}(a-1,b,c))\}.

Some of separations are depicted in Figure
31
.
Here,
\mathscr{G}_{(3)}(\mathsf{A}_{s})
is the
3
-graph which looks like the
2
-graph
\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{A}_{s})
.
Indeed, there are no edges in red and so the well-definedness of each mutation on
\mathscr{G}_{(3)}(\mathsf{A}_{s})
is the same as
\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{A}_{s})
.
Therefore we can safely replace
\mathscr{G}_{(3)}(\mathsf{A}_{s})
with
\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{A}_{s})
for the proof.

However, for each
1\leq j\leq\ell
, the
N
-subgraph
\mathscr{G}_{j}
is either
\mathscr{G}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime})
with
n^{\prime}=a^{\prime}+b^{\prime}+c^{\prime}-2
or
\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{A}_{n^{\prime}})
, where
n^{\prime}<n
.
Therefore the proposition follows from the induction on
n
once we establish the initial step, which is when
n=1
, that is, either

(\mathscr{G}(1,1,1),\mathscr{B}(1,1,1))\quad\text{ or }\quad(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{A}_{1}),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{A}_{1})).

Since there are no obstructions for mutations on these
N
-graphs, we are done for the initial condition for the induction.
∎

Remark 5.16
.

In the above proof, it is not claimed that two mutations
\mu^{\prime}
and
\mu_{\mathscr{G}}
commute.
Indeed, if we first mutate
(\mathscr{G}_{t_{0}},\mathscr{B}_{t_{0}})
via
\mu^{\prime}
, then the result may not look like either
(\mathscr{G}_{t_{0}},\mathscr{B}_{t_{0}})
or
(\bar{\mathscr{G}}_{t_{0}},\bar{\mathscr{B}}_{t_{0}})
and hence
\mu_{\mathscr{G}}
will not work as expected.

6.
Lagrangian fillings admitting cluster structures of type
\mathsf{BCFG}

In this section, we will construct cluster structures of type
\mathsf{BCFG}
on certain
N
-graphs by using the folding of
N
-graphs.
Throughout this section, let us assume that a triple
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y},G)
is one of

\displaystyle(\mathsf{A}_{2n-1},\mathsf{B}_{n},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}),

\displaystyle(\mathsf{D}_{n+1},\mathsf{C}_{n},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}),

\displaystyle(\mathsf{E}_{6},\mathsf{F}_{4},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}),

\displaystyle(\mathsf{D}_{4},\mathsf{G}_{2},\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z})

and that the group
G
is generated by
\tau
.

Remark 6.1
.

For
\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{D}_{n+1})=\mathcal{Q}(n-1,2,2),\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{E}_{6})=\mathcal{Q}(2,3,3)
and
\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{D}_{4})=\mathcal{Q}(2,2,2)
, we will use the labelling convention of tripods depicted in Figure
25(a)
, which is different from the usual convention of Dynkin diagrams shown in Table
1
.

6.1.
Rotations and
N
-graphs of type
\mathsf{A}_{2n-1}
and
\mathsf{D}_{4}

Let
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
be a pair of a
2
- or
3
-graph and a good tuple of cycles of type
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{A}_{2n-1}
or
\mathsf{D}_{4}
, respectively.
We define a new pair
(\tau(\mathscr{G}),\tau(\mathscr{B}))
such that

\tau(\mathscr{G})
and
\tau(\mathscr{B})
are obtained by the
(2\pi/N)
-rotation on
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
.

(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}

(a)

\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-action on
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
of type
\mathsf{A}_{2n-1}

(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}

(b)

\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}
-action on
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
of type
\mathsf{D}_{4}

Figure 32.
Rotation actions on
N
-graphs of type
\mathsf{A}_{2n-1}
and
\mathsf{D}_{4}

We say that
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
is
G
-admissible
if

(1)

the
N
-graph
\mathscr{G}
has the
(2\pi/N)
-rotation symmetry so that
\tau(\mathscr{G})=\mathscr{G}
,

(2)

the tuples of cycles
\mathscr{B}
and
\tau(\mathscr{B})
are identical up to relabelling as follows: if
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{A}_{2n-1}
,

\displaystyle\gamma_{i}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\gamma_{2n-i},

and if
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{D}_{4}
,

\displaystyle\gamma_{2}

\displaystyle\to\gamma_{3},

\displaystyle\gamma_{3}

\displaystyle\to\gamma_{4},

\displaystyle\gamma_{4}

\displaystyle\to\gamma_{2}.

In particular,
\tau
preserves
\gamma_{n}
if
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{A}_{2n-1}
and
\gamma_{1}
if
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{D}_{4}
.

33
shows examples and non-examples of
G
-admissible
N
-graphs.

N

non-

Remark 6.2
.

Notice that the Coxeter padding
\mathscr{C}
for
\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{A}_{2n-1})
is empty and
\mathscr{C}(2,2,2)
has obviously the
\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}
-rotational symmetry.
For each
G
-admissible
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
of type
\mathsf{A}_{2n-1}
or
\mathsf{D}_{4}
,
so is the following

\mathscr{C}\cdots\bar{\mathscr{C}}\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})\quad\text{ or }\quad\bar{\mathscr{C}}\cdots\bar{\mathscr{C}}\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B}).

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a quiver of type
\mathsf{A}_{2n-1}
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Q}
is invariant under the action

\displaystyle\tau(i)

\displaystyle=2n-i

for all
i\in[2n-1]
.
Then there is no oriented cycle of the form

j\to i\to\tau(j)\to\tau(i)\to j

for any
i,j\neq n
.

Proof.

It is well known that any minimal cycle in
\mathcal{Q}
is of length 3.
Therefore, if such an oriented cycle exists, then there must be an edge
i-\tau(i)
or
j-\tau(j)
in
\mathcal{Q}
. Hence
b_{i,\tau(i)}\neq 0
or
b_{j,\tau(j)}\neq 0
for
\mathcal{B}=(b_{k,\ell})=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{Q})
.

This is impossible because
\mathcal{Q}
is
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-admissible and so

b_{i,\tau(i)}=b_{\tau(i),\tau(\tau(i))}=b_{\tau(i),i}=-b_{i,\tau(i)}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad b_{i,\tau(i)}=0.

Therefore we are done.
∎

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
be of type
\mathsf{A}_{2n-1}
as above. If
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
is
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-admissible, then so is the quiver
\mathcal{Q}(\Lambda(\mathscr{G}),\mathscr{B})
.

Proof.

For
\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{Q}(\Lambda(\mathscr{G}),\mathscr{B})
, since the generator
\tau\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
acts on
\mathscr{B}
as
\tau(\gamma_{i})=\gamma_{2n-i}
, we have the
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-action on the set
[2n-1]
of vertices of
\mathcal{Q}
as
\tau(i)=2n-i
and therefore for each
i\in[2n-1]
,

i\sim 2n-i.

We will check the conditions (a), (b), (c), and (d) for admissibility according to Definition
3.10
.

(a) Since all vertices in
\mathcal{Q}
are mutable, the condition (a) is obviously satisfied.

(b) Let
\mathcal{B}=(b_{i,j})=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{Q})
. Then for each
i,j\in[2n-1]
, the entry
b_{i,j}
is given by the algebraic intersection number
(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j})
, which is the same as
(\gamma_{2n-i},\gamma_{2n-j})
since
\mathscr{G}
has the
\pi
-rotation symmetry.
Hence

b_{i,j}=b_{\tau(i),\tau(j)}.

(c) On the other hand, for each
i\in[2n-i]
, we have

b_{i,\tau(i)}=(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{\tau(i)})=(\gamma_{\tau(i)},\gamma_{\tau(\tau(i))})=(\gamma_{\tau(i)},\gamma_{i})=-b_{i,\tau(i)},

which implies that

b_{i,\tau(i)}=0.

(d) Finally, we need to prove that for each
i,j
,

b_{i,j}b_{\tau(i),j}\geq 0.

If
j=n
, then since
\tau(n)=n
, we have

b_{i,n}b_{\tau(i),n}=b_{i,n}b_{\tau(i),\tau(n)}=b_{i,n}b_{i,n}\geq 0.

Similarly, if
i=n
, then

b_{n,j}b_{\tau(n),j}=b_{n,j}b_{n,j}\geq 0.

Suppose that for some
i,j\neq n
,

b_{i,j}b_{\tau(i),j}<0.

By changing the roles of
i
and
\tau(i)
if necessary, we may assume that
b_{i,j}<0<b_{\tau(i),j}
.
Then we also have

b_{\tau(i),\tau(j)}<0<b_{i,\tau(j)},

which implies that there is an oriented cycle in
\mathcal{Q}

j\to i\to\tau(j)\to\tau(i)\to j.

However, this contradicts to Lemma
6.3
and therefore
\mathcal{Q}
satisfies all conditions in Definition
3.10
.
∎

Similarly, we have the following proposition as well.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
be of type
\mathsf{D}_{4}
. If
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
is
\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}
-admissible, then so is the quiver
\mathcal{Q}(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{Q}(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
. Then by definition of the
\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}
-action, we have

2\sim 3\sim 4.

(a) and (b) This is obvious as before.

(c) Let
\mathcal{B}=(b_{i,j})=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{Q})
. Suppose that
b_{2,3}\neq 0
. Then by (b),

b_{2,3}=b_{3,4}=b_{4,2}\neq 0

and so
\mathcal{Q}
has a directed cycle either

2\to 3\to 4\to 2\quad\text{or}\quad 2\to 4\to 3\to 2.

Then according to
b_{1,2}
, the underlying graph of the quiver
\mathcal{Q}
is either the complete graph
K_{4}
or a disconnected graph, but both are impossible. Therefore

b_{2,3}=b_{3,4}=b_{4,2}=0.

(d) The only entries we need to check are
b_{1,j}
’s, which are all equal by (b). Therefore

b_{1,j}b_{1,j^{\prime}}\geq 0.\qed

6.2.
Partial rotations and
N
-graphs of type
\mathsf{D}_{n+1}
and
\mathsf{E}_{6}

Recall from Table
5
that
the Legendrians
\lambda(\mathsf{D}_{n+1})=\lambda(n-1,2,2)
and
\lambda(\mathsf{E}_{6})=\lambda(2,3,3)
whose braid representatives are

\displaystyle\beta(\mathsf{D}_{n+1})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}^{n}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}^{3}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}^{3},

\displaystyle\beta(\mathsf{E}_{6})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}^{3}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}^{4}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}^{4}.

Let us identify
\mathbb{D}^{2}
with the unit disk in
\mathbb{C}
and define the ray
R_{\theta}
in
\mathbb{D}^{2}
as

R_{\theta}=\{(r,\theta)\in\mathbb{D}\subset\mathbb{C}\mid 0\leq r\leq 1\}.

In each case, we assume that three
\sigma_{2}
’s are on the end points of three rays
R_{0},R_{2\pi/3}
and
R_{4\pi/3}
, which are points
\{1,e^{2\pi/3},e^{4\pi/3}\}\subset S^{1}\subset\mathbb{C}
.
We also assume that two same blocks of
\sigma_{1}^{*}
in each
\lambda
are contained in the angle
[2\pi/3,4\pi/3]
and
[4\pi/3,2\pi]
.

Let
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
be a
3
-graph of type
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{D}_{n+1}
or
\mathsf{E}_{6}
.
We consider the intersection
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})\cap R_{\theta}
between
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
with the ray
R_{\theta}
, which consists of colored points or intervals possibly together with labels
\gamma_{j}
.

Definition 6.6

(Ray symmetry)
.

We say that the pair
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
is
ray-symmetric
if the intersections
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})\cap R_{\theta}
for
\theta=0,2\pi/3,4\pi/3
are the same up to rotation and avoid all trivalent vertices and hexagonal points except at the origin.

(R_{0},(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})\cap R_{0})\cong(R_{2\pi/3},(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})\cap R_{2\pi/3})\cong(R_{4\pi/3},(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})\cap R_{4\pi/3}).

(6.1)

R_{0}
R_{2\pi/3}
R_{4\pi/3}

(R_{0},(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})\cap R_{0})=
(R_{2\pi/3},(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})\cap R_{2\pi/3})=
(R_{4\pi/3},(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})\cap R_{4\pi/3})=
\cong
\cong

Figure 34.
Ray-symmetricity

Then we define a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-action on a ray-symmetric
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
as follows:

(1)

cut
\mathbb{D}^{2}
into three sectors
\mathbb{D}^{2}_{1},\mathbb{D}^{2}_{2}
and
\mathbb{D}^{2}_{3}
along the rays
R_{\theta}
for
\theta=0,2\pi/3
and
4\pi/3
so that
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
gives us three
3
-subgraphs

\displaystyle\{(\mathscr{G}_{1},\mathscr{B}_{1}),(\mathscr{G}_{2},\mathscr{B}_{2}),(\mathscr{G}_{3},\mathscr{B}_{3})\},

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{G}\cap\mathbb{D}^{2}_{i},

(2)

change two subgraphs contained in sectors whose angles are in between
[2\pi/3,4\pi/3]
and
[4\pi/3,2\pi]
by rotating certain angles.

(3)

The result will be denoted by
(\tau(\mathscr{G}),\tau(\mathscr{B}))
.

Notice that each subgraph
\mathscr{G}_{i}
may not satisfy the condition of
N
-graphs but the final result will be an well-defined
3
-graph since
\mathscr{G}
is ray-symmetric.
However, if
\mathscr{G}
is not ray-symmetric, then the
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-action is never well-defined.
We call this action the
partial rotation
and see Figure
35
for the pictorial definition.

(\mathscr{G}_{1},\mathscr{B}_{1})
(\mathscr{G}_{2},\mathscr{B}_{2})
(\mathscr{G}_{3},\mathscr{B}_{3})

(\mathscr{G}_{2},\mathscr{B}_{2})

(\mathscr{G}_{2},\mathscr{B}_{2})

(\mathscr{G}_{1},\mathscr{B}_{1})
(\mathscr{G}_{2},\mathscr{B}_{2})
(\mathscr{G}_{3},\mathscr{B}_{3})

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}

Figure 35.
Partial rotation

We say that
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
is
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-admissible if it is invariant under the partial rotation up to relabeling of cycles as follows:

(1)

if
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{D}_{n+1}
, then

\gamma_{n}\leftrightarrow\gamma_{n+1},

(2)

if
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{E}_{6}
, then

\displaystyle\gamma_{3}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\gamma_{5},

\displaystyle\gamma_{4}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\gamma_{6}.

Remark 6.7
.

Similar to Remark
6.2
, the Coxeter padding
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}(n-1,2,2)
for
\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{D}_{n+1})
or
\mathscr{C}(2,3,3)
for
\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{E}_{6})
has also the
\mathbb{Z}/2Z
-symmetry under the partial rotation.
Therefore for any
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-admissible
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
under the partial rotation of type
\mathsf{D}_{n+1}
or
\mathsf{E}_{6}
, so is the following

\mathscr{C}\cdots\bar{\mathscr{C}}\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})\quad\text{ or }\quad\bar{\mathscr{C}}\cdots\bar{\mathscr{C}}\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B}).

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a quiver of type
\mathsf{E}_{6}
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Q}
is invariant under the action

\displaystyle\tau(3)

\displaystyle=5,

\displaystyle\tau(4)

\displaystyle=6.

Then there is no oriented cycle, which is either

3\to 4\to 5\to 6\to 3\quad\text{or}\quad 3\to 6\to 5\to 4\to 3.

Proof.

We will use the essentially same argument as the proof of Lemma
6.3
.

If
\mathcal{Q}=\mu(\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{E}_{6}))
, then by Proposition
3.15
and Lemma
3.16
, there exist an integer
r
and a sequence
\mu^{\prime}
of mutations such that

\mathcal{Q}=\mu^{\prime}(\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{r}(\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{E}_{6})))=\mu^{\prime}(\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{E}_{6})).

Moreover,
\mu^{\prime}
misses at least one mutation
\mu_{i}
.

(1)

If
i=1
, then
\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{E}_{6})\setminus\{1\}
consists of three quivers

\displaystyle\{2\}

\displaystyle\subset\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{A}_{1}),

\displaystyle\{3,4\}

\displaystyle\subset\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{A}_{2}),

\displaystyle\{5,6\}

\displaystyle\subset\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{A}_{2}).

In particular, there are no direct edges between two sets of vertices
\{3,4\}
and
\{5,6\}
.

(2)

If
i=2
, then
\mu^{\prime}
can be regarded as a sequence of mutations of type
\mathsf{A}_{5}
. By Lemma
6.3
, we are done.

(3)

If
i=3
, then we may assume that
\mu^{\prime}
also misses
\mu_{5}
due to the symmetry. Hence we have separated quivers

\displaystyle\{1,2\}

\displaystyle\subset\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{A}_{2}),

\displaystyle\{4\}

\displaystyle\subset\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{A}_{1}),

\displaystyle\{6\}

\displaystyle\subset\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{A}_{1}).

Then after the mutation, the vertices
4
and
6
can be joined only with
3
and
5
, respectively, and so we never have an edge between
3
and
6
or between
4
to
5
.

(4)

If
i=4
, then as above, we may assume that
\mu^{\prime}
misses
\mu_{6}
as well and we may regard
\mu^{\prime}
as a sequence of mutations on
\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{D}_{4})

\{1,2,3,5\}\subset\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{D}_{4}).

Moreover,
\mu^{\prime}
consists of mutations corresponding to
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-orbits, which are
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
and
\mu_{3,5}=(\mu_{3}\mu_{5})
.
Here the group
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
folds
\mathsf{D}_{4}
onto
\mathsf{C}_{3}
, and up to Coxeter mutations, there are only three facets

\displaystyle F_{-\alpha_{1}}^{\mathsf{C}_{3}}

\displaystyle\cong P(\Phi(\mathsf{C}_{2})),

\displaystyle F_{-\alpha_{2}}^{\mathsf{C}_{3}}

\displaystyle\cong P(\Phi(\mathsf{A}_{1}))\times P(\Phi(\mathsf{A}_{1})),

\displaystyle F_{-\alpha_{3}}^{\mathsf{C}_{3}}

\displaystyle\cong P(\Phi(\mathsf{A}_{2})),

Hence all possible quivers are obtained by one of the following ways:

\displaystyle\underbrace{\mu_{1}\mu_{3,5}\mu_{1}\cdots}_{k}(\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{D}_{4})),

\displaystyle 0\leq k

\displaystyle\leq 11

\displaystyle\underbrace{\mu_{2}\mu_{3,5}\mu_{2}\cdots}_{k}(\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{D}_{4})),

\displaystyle 0\leq k

\displaystyle\leq 3

\displaystyle\underbrace{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{1}\cdots}_{k}(\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{D}_{4})),

\displaystyle 0\leq k

\displaystyle\leq 9

Note that two quivers

(\mu_{1}\mu_{3,5}\mu_{1}\mu_{3,5}\mu_{1}\mu_{3,5})(\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{D}_{4}))\quad\text{ and }\quad(\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{1})(\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{D}_{4}))

are obtained from
\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{D}_{4}))
by permuting vertices
3\leftrightarrow 5
and
1\leftrightarrow 2
, respectively.
Finally, one can directly check that we have no such cycles by the exhaustive search in this full list.∎

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
be of type
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{D}_{n+1}
or
\mathsf{E}_{6}
.
If
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
is
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-admissible, then so is the quiver
\mathcal{Q}(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
.

Proof.

(a) and (b): This is obvious as before.

(c) Let
\mathcal{B}=(b_{i,j})=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{Q})
. Then by (b),

b_{i,\tau(i)}=b_{\tau(i),\tau(\tau(i))}=b_{\tau(i),i}=-b_{i,\tau(i)}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad b_{i,\tau(i)}=0.

(d) If
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{D}_{n+1}
, then we only need to show

b_{i,n}b_{i,n+1}\geq 0

for
i<n
. This is obvious since

b_{i,n+1}=b_{\tau(i),\tau(n+1)}=b_{i,n}.

If
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{E}_{6}
, then all we need to show are inequalities

\displaystyle b_{i,j}b_{i,j+2}

\displaystyle\geq 0,

\displaystyle b_{3,4}b_{3,6}

\displaystyle\geq 0

for
i=1,2
and
j=3,4
.

The first inequality is obvious since

\displaystyle b_{i,j+2}

\displaystyle=b_{\tau(i),\tau(j+2)}=b_{i,j}.

Suppose that
b_{3,4}b_{3,6}<0
. Then since
b_{3,4}=b_{5,6}
and
b_{3,6}=b_{5,4}
, the
\mathcal{Q}
has a loop either

3\to 4\to 5\to 6\to 3\quad\text{or}\quad 3\to 6\to 5\to 4\to 3.\qed

6.2.1.
\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{A}_{2n-1})
as a tripod
\mathcal{Q}(1,n,n)

As observed in Lemma
5.3
, one can think
\mathcal{Q}(1,n,n)
and
\mathscr{G}(1,n,n)
for
\mathsf{A}_{2n-1}
instead of
\mathcal{Q}(\mathsf{A}_{2n-1})
and
\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{A}_{2n-1})
.

The major difference is now we have to use
3
-graphs and partial rotations instead of
2
-graphs and
\pi
-rotations.
Then it can be easily checked that the above two notions are identical and so the
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-admissibility for
3
-graphs of type
\mathsf{A}_{2n-1}
is also well-defined.
Moreover, the
3
-graph analogue under the partial rotation of Proposition
6.4
will be true.

6.3.
Global foldability of
N
-graphs

Let
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
be of type
\mathsf{X}
. We say that
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
is
globally foldable
with respect to
G
if
(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{B})
is
G
-admissible and for any sequence of mutable
G
-orbits
I_{1},\dots,I_{\ell}
, there exists a
G
-admissible
(\mathscr{G}^{\prime},\mathscr{B}^{\prime})
such that

\mathcal{Q}(\Lambda(\mathscr{G}^{\prime}),\mathscr{B}^{\prime})=(\mu_{I_{\ell}}\cdots\mu_{I_{1}})(\mathcal{Q}(\Lambda(\mathscr{G}),\mathscr{B})).

Theorem 6.10
.

The
N
-graph with a good tuple of cycles
(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{X}),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{X}))
is globally foldable with respect to
G
.

Proof.

Let us define the initial quiver
\mathcal{Q}_{t_{0}}
by

\mathcal{Q}_{t_{0}}=\mathcal{Q}(\Lambda(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{X})),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{X})).

For a sequence of mutable
G
-orbits
I_{1},\dots,I_{\ell}
, we have an integer
r
and
\mu^{\prime\mathsf{Y}}
by Proposition
3.15
and Lemma
3.16
such that in the cluster pattern of type
\mathsf{Y}
, two sequences of mutations

\left((\mu_{I_{\ell}}^{\mathsf{X}}\cdots\mu_{I_{1}}^{\mathsf{X}})(\mathcal{Q}_{t_{0}})\right)^{G}=\mu^{\prime\mathsf{Y}}\left((\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathsf{Y}})^{r}\left(\mathcal{Q}_{t_{0}}^{G}\right)\right)

will produce the same seed.

On the other hand, as seen in Remark
3.19
, the Coxeter mutation
\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathsf{X}}
will correspond to the Coxeter mutation
\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathsf{Y}}
via the folding.
Moreover,
\mu^{\prime\mathsf{Y}}
comes from a sequence
\mu^{\prime\mathsf{X}}
of mutations via the folding such that
\mu^{\prime\mathsf{X}}
is the composition of mutations at
G
-orbits and happens inside some facet
F_{\beta}\subset P(\Phi(\mathsf{X}))
.
Hence we have

\displaystyle\mu^{\prime\mathsf{Y}}\left((\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathsf{Y}})^{r}\left(\mathcal{Q}_{t_{0}}^{G}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\mu^{\prime\mathsf{Y}}\left(((\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathsf{X}})^{r}(\mathcal{Q}_{t_{0}}))^{G}\right)=\left(\mu^{\prime\mathsf{X}}((\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathsf{X}})^{r}(\mathcal{Q}_{t_{0}}))\right)^{G}.

By Proposition
5.15
, there exists a pair
(\mathscr{G}^{\prime},\mathscr{B}^{\prime})
satisfying that

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(\Lambda(\mathscr{G}^{\prime}),\mathscr{B}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}(\mu^{\prime\mathsf{X}}((\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathsf{X}})^{r}(\mathcal{Q}_{t_{0}})))=(\mu_{I_{\ell}}^{\mathsf{X}}\cdots\mu_{I_{1}}^{\mathsf{X}})(\mathcal{Q}_{t_{0}}).

Finally, we need to show that
(\mathscr{G}^{\prime},\mathscr{B}^{\prime})
can be assumed to be
G
-admissible.
As in the proof of Proposition
5.15
,
(\mathscr{G}^{\prime},\mathscr{B}^{\prime})
is obtained by taking a
\mu^{\prime\mathsf{X}}
on either
(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{X}),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{X}))
or
(\bar{\mathscr{G}}(\mathsf{X}),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{X}))
and attaching Coxeter paddings.
As observed in Remarks
6.2
and
6.7
, the Coxeter paddings themselves are already
G
-admissible and the process attaching them preserve the
G
-admissibility in each case.
Therefore we only need to show the
G
-admissibility of
\mu^{\prime\mathsf{X}}(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{X}),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{X}))
.

We will use the essentially same strategy as the proof of Proposition
5.15
.
Since
\mu^{\prime\mathsf{X}}
misses some
\mu_{\gamma_{i}}
, it misses all
\mu_{\gamma_{i^{\prime}}}
for
i\sim i^{\prime}
.
Then one can split
(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{X}),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{X}))
in a
G
-admissible way. That is, the set of
N
-subgraphs

\{(\mathscr{G}_{1},\mathscr{B}_{1}),\dots,(\mathscr{G}_{\ell},\mathscr{B}_{\ell})\}

is closed under the
G
-action. In this case,
G
may permute
N
-subgraphs as well.
Now we split
\mu^{\prime\mathsf{X}}
into
\{\mu^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\mu^{\prime}_{\ell}\}
such that each
\mu^{\prime}_{i}
is a sequence of mutations of
\mathcal{Q}(\mathscr{G}_{i},\mathscr{B}_{i})
.
Then
\mu^{\prime\mathsf{X}}(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{X}),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{X}))
is
G
-admissible if so is
\mu^{\prime}_{i}(\mathscr{G}_{i},\mathscr{B}_{i})
for each
1\leq i\leq\ell
.

Since each
(\mathscr{G}_{i},\mathscr{B}_{i})
is strictly simpler than
(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{X}),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{X}))
in terms of the number of vertices and is again of type
\mathsf{ADE}
, the rest of the proof follows from induction and we omit the detail.
∎

As a direct consequence, we will prove the following theorem:

Theorem 6.11
.

The following holds:

(1)

The Legendrian link
\lambda(\mathsf{A}_{2n-1})
has
\binom{2n}{n}

\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-admissible
N
-graphs which admits the cluster pattern of type
\mathsf{B}_{n}
.

(2)

The Legendrian link
\lambda(\mathsf{D}_{n+1})
has
\binom{2n}{n}

\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-admissible
N
-graphs which admits the cluster pattern of type
\mathsf{C}_{n}
.

(3)

The Legendrian link
\lambda(\mathsf{E}_{6})
has
105

\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-admissible
N
-graphs which admits the cluster pattern of type
\mathsf{F}_{4}
.

(4)

The Legendrian link
\lambda(\mathsf{D}_{4})
has
8

\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}
-admissible
N
-graphs which admits the cluster pattern of type
\mathsf{G}_{2}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
6.10
, it is already known that for each
\mathsf{X}
, the quiver of type
\mathsf{X}
is globally foldable with respect to
G
.
By Propositions
6.4
,
6.5
and
6.9
, the quiver
\mathcal{Q}(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{X}),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{X}))
is also globally foldable with respect to
G
.

Let
\Sigma_{t_{0}}=\Psi(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{X}),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{X}),\mathcal{F}_{\lambda})=(\mathbf{x}(\Lambda(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{X})),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{X}),\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}),\mathcal{Q}(\Lambda(\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{X})),\mathscr{B}(\mathsf{X})))
be the initial seed.
Without loss of generality, we may denote cluster variables in
\mathbf{x}
by

\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{\operatorname{rk}(\mathsf{X})})

and we define a field homomorphism
\psi:\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}(x_{1},\dots,x_{\operatorname{rk}(\mathsf{X})})\to\mathbb{F}^{G}=\mathbb{C}(x_{I_{1}},\dots,x_{I_{\operatorname{rk}(\mathsf{Y})}})
by

\psi(x_{i})=x_{I}

for any
i
in a
G
-orbit
I
.
Then by construction, the initial seed
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
is
(G,\psi)
-admissible. See §
3.3
.

Finally, by Proposition
3.13
, folded seeds form a seed pattern of type
\mathsf{Y}
as desired, and we are done.
∎
